- 2026-11-06 decision D10.14: one standard policy in the defaults; retries a network failure, 408, 429, 502, 503, 504; never another 4xx, never 500, 501, 505 (the app's own exception repeats); only GET, PUT, DELETE (RFC 9110 9.2.2); .NET's standard handler's numbers (3 retries, exponential with jitter from 2 s, Retry-After, 30 s in all; learn.microsoft.com/dotnet/core/resilience/http-resilience). Disagrees with the owner's example on 500, with the reason.

- 2026-11-06 owner: "I concede my point on retry. Just implement the standard around HTTP status codes. Or we could literally say that retry after header has to be sent." Answer, D10.14: the standard status set stays; Retry-After governs the wait when sent (seconds, or its date against the response's Date), a wait past the 30 s budget ends the retries, and a malformed value is logged once and the standard wait runs. Retry-After-only is refused: a network failure and a gateway's 502 or 504 carry no header, and they are what a retry is for.

Fact: ASP.NET Core's rate limiter rejects with 503 by default and adds Retry-After only when the app writes it in OnRejected, and its concurrency limiter writes an empty 503 (learn.microsoft.com RateLimiterOptions.RejectionStatusCode, ConcurrencyLimiterOptions.OnRejected): a 503 without the header is common and transient, and D10.14's standard wait covers it, as it covers a network failure, 502 and 504.

- 2026-11-06 decision: the prove-behavior skill (.claude/skills/prove-behavior/SKILL.md) holds the judgment: which defect, what its verdict means, what the report carries. The mechanics are scripts/plant-defect.sh:
  - it plants one text found once and runs the filtered tests under a unique stamp, so the verdict reads a fresh TRX;
  - its verdict keys on the named test (caught, with the failure message and what else failed, or ESCAPED), and it refuses a run where that test did not run once;
  - it restores the file by copy and touch on every exit, rebuilding the assets;
  - it refuses a defect in the test project.
  A project gives the tool its asset build in its descriptor (playwright_build_assets). Only poc declares one; the tool refuses the other three projects by that name. WhenARuleNamesTheCode now reads a skill's SKILL.md, so a script, option or name the skill cites is held. Proof:
  - caught, ESCAPED and both refusals were seen on the poc project;
  - a run killed with TERM mid-test restored the file (git diff clean, mtime moved), with no test process or listener left;
  - a planted --name in the skill failed WhenARuleNamesTheCode ("scripts/plant-defect.sh reads no option --name"), and the restored skill passed it, 8 of 8.

They stay while the skill is only on feature/spec-first, since every worktree shares that memory, and they are removed when it lands on main.

- 2026-11-06 finding: the plant "a digit segment of a vendor path is a member step" ESCAPED. JavaScript reads a[0] and a["0"] alike, and the runtime reads both through `in` and Reflect.get, so on a vendor path the index step changes only the plan's shape. Whether the index step has a meaning of its own (a position read only on an array) is decided at POC 4 by a real array case. If it has none, it leaves the grammar and a digit segment becomes a member step.

- 2026-11-06 correction to the POC 3 finding "no exception to D18.4". Two probes (scratch console, net10, the reflection resolver) found two defects.
  - Overrides: for d => d.Room, where Room overrides Person's virtual property, the expression carries Person.Room and the contract holds Resident.Room. The identity match fails, and the read is refused with a false message. Inherited members (Person.Name) and members hidden by new (Resident.Ward) match. Matching an override needs MemberInfo.Name, which the ban refuses, so it is a gate-1 decision (D18.3): an owner-listed exception, or refusing such a read.
  - net48: GetTypeInfo on options with no resolver throws NotSupportedException, and net48's branch builds Web defaults with none, so every read of data would have failed at render. Fixed at the boundary that takes the app's options: PlanBuild calls MakeReadOnly(populateMissingResolver: true), which the probe shows fills the resolver and accepts options that are already read-only. This is unobservable in the browser on net10, where MVC's options carry a resolver, and net48 is not run.

- 2026-11-06 owner, FYI and not a direction: conditions could have been plain C# with the primitives the framework supports (if, else if, else, one level of nesting), and the model could carry pure methods into the plan as plain JSON; Razor views do not talk well with analyzers, which is why parameters are passed down the stack in the fluent syntax. The spec holds When, ElseIf and Else in authored order, first match wins (D8.2, D8.6, X8.4), with branches as blocks that nest to any depth (D8.7, D8.8). Facts for the idea: a C# if is a statement, and a statement lambda has no expression tree (CS0834), so only an expression lambda (m => m.Age >= 65 && m.Wing == "East") can be carried, which is the lambda door D8.2 already gives array predicates and validator conditions; a Razor if runs on the server when the view renders, never in the browser. By default MVC 5 compiles a view when it is first requested, so a build-time analyzer does not see a net48 view; precompilation (MvcBuildViews, or a generator such as RazorGenerator.MsBuild) is opt-in and not probed. What an analyzer must check on both hosts therefore lives in .cs files (models, components, validators). A pure model method carried into the plan is main's PureMethodAttribute scan (B11, D18.3 at gate 1); without reflection it needs a source generator over the model's .cs file. The passing down the stack is the fluent syntax's to fix (the condensed syntax the owner asked for, NOTES above), at gate 2.

The alternates for naming a member a lambda reaches through an override, with a probe (scratch console, net10, both the reflection resolver and a JsonSerializerContext, eight cases: inherited, abstract override, an override in a middle class, new, own, an override of a generic base, an interface member, a struct member):
  - the base-type walk (in the code): the right JSON name in all 16;
  - the member's declaring class (MemberInfo.DeclaringType): the same name in all 16, one read instead of a walk; it is in System.Reflection, so the POC's namespace ban refuses it, and D14.3's element ids read it anyway;
  - refuse a read through an override: no type read, and the abstract-base case the owner keeps is lost;
  - the member's C# name matched to the contract: a C# name mapped to a JSON name, which is the contract's own job; not taken;
  - build time: a source generator cannot see Razor's generated views, and an analyzer can refuse at build but cannot hand a name to the render.
  No alternate keeps the override case without a type read: the compiler names the declaration, the derived class's contract lists the override, and joining them needs the class that declares the member. The owner decides between the walk and the declaring class.

So since you mentioned the reflection optimization are quite good, I would allow that, but please think through if there is a better alternate available which is more stabilized and give us better performance." Answer: the alternates are compiling per value (1000 times slower and generates code at render), a cache of compiled accessors (what MVC's own internal CachedExpressionCompiler does for model expressions; 20 times slower than the reflection read here, plus code generation, a cache and its concurrency), and a build-time generator (none sees Razor's generated views, and net48 views compile without generators). The member-chain read with FieldInfo.GetValue and PropertyInfo.GetValue is the fastest measured, generates no code, holds no cache and is the same API on net48 and net10. Decision D6.7: that read, for fields and properties only; a view value that calls a method or computes is refused, naming the local it goes into. - 2026-11-06 owner: "for decimal use normal finance standard bascially what banks use or have it configured at global level". Answer: decimal Sum is decimal arithmetic (BigInt whole numbers at the largest scale, the point placed once), exact as .NET's decimal addition, so nothing rounds; the first operation that rounds a decimal takes one app-wide mode, MidpointRounding.ToEven by default (.NET's own and banker's rounding), AwayFromZero by choice. Limit stated: a decimal sent as a JSON number keeps about 15 significant digits when parsed; past that the app sends decimals as text. D6.11, X6.9.

Answer, disagreeing on one point with the reason: the knobs (places, default 2; rounding, default ToEven, AwayFromZero by choice) apply where an operation must round; sums and comparisons stay exact, because medication doses carry three places (digoxin 0.125 mg, levothyroxine 0.088 mg) and a browser that rounds 0.125 to 0.12 or 0.13 disagrees with the server's decimal. Waiting on the owner's yes.

- 2026-11-06 probe (scratchpad text-probe, .NET 10.0.1 with ICU and headless Chromium 153, every code point U+0000 to U+10FFFF but the surrogates): char.IsWhiteSpace accepts 25 characters, JavaScript's trim removes 25; .NET alone has U+0085, the browser alone U+FEFF. Upper case, invariant, en-US and tr-TR: JavaScript maps 102 code points to more than one where .NET maps to one (ß, the ligatures, the Greek letters with a subscript iota); .NET keeps 75 of them and maps 27 to another letter (U+1F80 to U+1F87, U+1F90 to U+1F97, U+1FA0 to U+1FA7, U+1FB3, U+1FC3, U+1FF3); invariant upper case keeps ı (U+0131), which JavaScript maps to I. Lower case: İ (U+0130) is two code points in JavaScript, kept by .NET's invariant and i under en-US; in a whole string JavaScript lowers a final Σ to ς, .NET to σ. The rule in translated-members.md (each code point mapped alone, a mapping to more than one replaced by .NET's from the 27-letter table or the code point itself, ı kept in invariant upper case) differs from .NET on none of the 1,112,064 in any of the six casings. Node 24 agrees with Chromium on all of it. With CurrentCulture en-US: "\u00ADward".StartsWith("ward") is true, "ward".StartsWith("\u00AD") is true, "e\u0301".StartsWith("\u00E9") is true, "ward".EndsWith("\u00ADd") is true, and "ward".Contains("\u00AD") is false: StartsWith(string) and EndsWith(string) compare by the culture, Contains(string) is ordinal. net48 not run.

On net48 t => t.TrimStart() carries TrimStart(char[]) with an empty array, the same key as TrimStart('x'); the table translates that key only with an empty array (translated-members.md). t.Contains('x') binds to Enumerable.Contains<char> on net48, for POC 4 unit 3b-ii's row of fixed values to refuse when the values read the item.

- 2026-11-06 finding (Microsoft Learn: CompareOptions remarks, Best practices for comparing strings, .NET globalization and ICU): .NET's culture order differs between its own hosts. net48 compares with NLS word sort, where a hyphen or an apostrophe weighs little (billet, bills, bill's, cannot, cant, can't, con, coop, co-op); net10 compares with ICU, where None is StringSort (bill's, billet, bills, can't, cannot, cant, co-op, con, coop); NLS counts a ligature equal to its letters, ICU does not; the ICU version on the host decides the weights; invariant globalization mode compares every culture ordinally. Ordinal never changes. So OrderBy over a text key has no one .NET meaning to match on both hosts: the owner's Decision 3.

Binding {"settings":{}} reads a populated member from the holder's own start object ("ward-night") and a replaced one from a new object of its type (null); a getter-only member keeps the holder's object ("getter"). A nested holder that cannot be created fails the bind (private constructor, abstract, interface: NotSupportedException), while the same holder absent binds null. A polymorphic holder binds {} as its base (Site null) and {"$type":"oral"} as the derived type (Site "mouth"), so its start depends on the data. Under RespectRequiredConstructorParameters a constructor parameter with no default is IsRequired; a copy with that option off and IsRequired false binds {} (Refills 3, Name null). A record whose constructor parameter's property is [JsonIgnore] binds {}; Populate on a parameterless class inside a positional record binds. So each step carries what the endpoint binds for the rest of the path when its member is absent: the value (a literal), nothing (a null holder on the way), or stop (a holder it cannot create, or a polymorphic one).

One rule replaces 10a-1's check for MVC's empty name and holds for inputs and partials alike; m => m (0 steps, "") passes it, and an input still refuses it for naming no member. A key holding "]" is counted wrongly, since MVC writes a position up to its first "]": such a key over a lambda MVC names whole counts more steps and is refused; only a key built to hold "]." over a call MVC cannot name could pass.

At boot it dispatches alis:mount on the document with { root: document.body } before it starts any plan (D12.3, D17.3). The app's components construct on alis:mount through a hook of the app's own code (Components/mount.ts, onMount(selector, construct)), never at script load; the redesign's components do the same (Alis.Reactive.Assets/components/hooks.ts at 65fdf118d).

- 2026-11-07 flags for the gate 1 report from 13c: (1) Syncfusion types every event `EmitType<T> = AngularEventEmitter & ((arg?: any, ...rest: any[]) => void)` (`@syncfusion/ej2-base/src/base.d.ts:8`), so `T` is phantom and a listener's first parameter is `any`: read exactly, every Fusion payload is ALIS021 (895 members in the loaded packages, 124 on DropDownList, MultiSelect, NumericTextBox, DatePicker and Grid). Options: read a type argument the member's declared alias does not use as the payload it documents, a rule of the type text (recommended, about 20 lines of the script); a payload declaration names its vendor type with `[Mirrors]`, which a duplicated name defeats; or `any` passes unchecked. (2) A reached declaration's diagnostics show on build, not as typed, since what reaches it can be anywhere in the compilation. (3) An `Answer(..)` a method takes reaches its arguments through the method's parameters, unchecked until argument agreement, and so are the parameters of a function `MethodAt` calls. (4) A declaration a referenced assembly holds is checked by the build that compiles it. (5) A namespace member reached on demand whose name a top-level declaration of its module holds is listed as `Name#n`, and `[Mirrors]` by that name resolves to the top-level one.

(2) A `Listen` on a component reached by its element's id is refused, since its events are `.Reactive`'s where it renders; whether that refusal is the door 14a-i's flag (3) asks for, a plan's handler on an element it does not render, is the owner's. (5) A write to a payload root declared as an `EventedObject` that is no component stays refused after an await and inside a `Listen` (D11.4); only a `Component` payload takes it. (6) An `EventedObject`'s listen is released `living`: it has no element to ask, so when its host destroys it and its `off` throws, the release calls that `off` whenever the browser has not yet collected the object or the listener, and skips it once it has; which of the two happens is the collector's. OPTIONS is RFC 9110's (section 9.3.7), fetch sends it (its forbidden methods are CONNECT, TRACE and TRACK), and what it answers is the `Allow` header, so it rests on the response's headers as a source. It is not CORS's preflight: a preflight carries `Access-Control-Request-Method`, which the app's request does not. To probe: whether IIS's `OPTIONSVerbHandler` answers before MVC 5 on net48, and whether net10's CORS middleware leaves an app OPTIONS to the action.

  - the condensed body is multipart: both, now D10.22, on `probe:core-json-to-form-bound` (JSON to a form-bound action binds nothing, silent) against `probe:core-frombody-multipart` (multipart to `[FromBody]` answers 415, loud).
  - a content reader per route, `Content.None`, `Json<T>()`, `Text`, `Html`, `File`, for success and error alike: A. B's kind-specific doors left error bodies JSON only, against the owner's "not everything will be JSON" (a 409's text, an invalid post's partial view).

  - defaults run per request, and the core counts the busy hold: B, as `Composition.tla` proves. A's defaults that run only at the count's edges change their meaning and would invalidate the model.

Two came from this unit's own first cut: Composition's `aborted` beside a new `stopped`, two booleans for one choice, and Lanes' `skipped` beside `replaced`, two booleans for the policy's one decision, the first of them steering `FireSecond`'s guard. - 2026-11-08 17c(a1), the run driver (D11.11, D11.12): `poc/runtime/src/execute.ts`'s promise chain (`Run = finished | awaiting`, the rest of a block run inside each promise's `then`) gives way to one driver. A run is a stack of blocks. One loop runs steps in the turn until one waits or the stack ends, and returns the turn's `waiting` or `ended`; only the driver attaches a continuation, and a stop or a rejection ends the run and is reported once, through its handler's callback (`objects.ts` attaches none). No plan change (version 11). Continuation sites go from execute.ts's three (an await's, a branch's, `carried`'s) and objects.ts's `.catch` to the driver's one `then`; `settle`'s own `then`, which maps a call's value to a read, stands. - 2026-11-09 gate 1's P4, decided within the owner's yes: a callback property's listeners chain (D17.3). The first listener of an object's property, a handler's or a `listen` step's, assigns one function of the framework's, which keeps the value it replaced; a later one joins its list with no write. The vendor's call runs the replaced value first, then each listener once in the order it subscribed (the page's plans in page order, one plan's handlers in its order, a slot's at its load, a nested slot's where its script sits, a `listen` step's when it runs, P5's on a page component included, a reload after the others), and returns what the replaced value returned, its throw reaching the vendor after every listener ran. A release removes its own listener from any place; the last puts the replaced value back only while the property holds the function, so a script's value stays; a script's assignment replaces every listener, and the next listener chains over the script's value. An object that lacks the property or refuses the assignment starts no handler and stops a `listen` step's pipeline, naming the object and property; a refused put-back is that listener's release error (P3), the function staying and calling the replaced value and no listener; the function is its object's, so a release after a restart writes nothing (D12.5). This answers 14a-i's flag (1) and reaches 14a-ii's A2: two owners on one property meet the put-back check. Calling the replaced value is the redesign's, its test "keeps calling the function the property already held, before the handler", and keeps what the vendor met before any listener; so D12.5's "which runs nothing" for a put-back that threw reads "runs no listener". No listener answers (D4.8, D17.12), so two handlers share no answer; an event on a function whose declared return takes no `undefined` is a callback, ALIS018 (X17.16, restated to that case); ALIS003 is refused, as 2026-11-08's first verdict had it, and N18.14 leaves. Not held by the model, each a rule a browser test proves: the error and the stop, the replaced value's return and throw reaching the vendor, its `this`.
